> @nickmoose He makes a few questionable statements that are likely to go unnoticed by younger people. For example, when he talks about religious people, he uses a popular technique where he makes two related statements, one after another, where the 1st one is true and easy to confirm, but the 2nd one is not true and hard to confirm. Most people fall for this cause the 1st one lowers their guard and they would not bother checking whether the 2nd one is also true.  
>   
> First he says that most people on Earth are religious: this is true, and easy to confirm, lots of polls out there. But the 2nd statement is false, where he says that "they take their religion very very seriously and they're willing to die for their religion". That's not the case, most people out there that consider themselves being "religious" don't take their religion seriously at all, and most definitely aren't willing to die for it. All those people are included in the 1st statistics (cause they answered "Yes" to the question "Are you a religious person, do you believe in God?"), but the vast majority of the so-called "religious" people aren't devoted to their religion, they don't go to church and don't pray regularly, and don't follow most of their religion's rules. This is especially true for Christianity. It's a complicated subject though that requires doing deeper research, so most people won't go into it. It's easy to confirm the 1st claim (that most people are religious), but a lot harder to research what people actually mean when they say they're religious. For most Christians, being religious means they wear an extra piece of jewelry (like a cross on their neck), try to be good (just like pretty much every other human being out there), and they might pray when in trouble. But going to wars, dying for their religion? Nope, not gonna happen. I mean, he even contradicts himself here: he used a pretty good metaphor for religions, comparing them to a "free lottery ticket". It is "free" because it doesn't take much to get into, but it certainly goes both ways - people easily accept religion cause "why not", and just as easily break most of its rules. Religious people drink and take drugs, lie, cheat on their spouses, beat their kids and do all kinds of nasty stuff prohibited by their religion. They don't care to abstain from all that, and most certainly don't care dying for some letters in some book. They consider themselves religious when that suits them. And that's the majority of the so-called "religious" people out there. The ones that are actually willing to die for their religion are a tiny minority. There's plenty of research on this, studying different groups of "religious" people, what it actually means for them to be religious etc.  
>   
> Some of the lists he makes are also questionable, like the list of things Iran needs to win a war against the US, for example. If I recall correctly, he listed 4 things, and it just looked weird. Not that they're "false", but there seems to be some selection bias there, it's like he's pushing a certain narrative and only picks the things that would help him with his narrative, omitting other important details. Why only 4 in that case? Why not 3 or 5? What about the economy, for example - it's not mentioned in that list at all, but Iran would certainly need to make some serious adjustments to their economy in order to win a full-scale invasion? Probably some internal political changes would be needed as well? But nope, only those 4 things need to be done according to this professor, and nothing else. It seems to be a recurring thing with his lectures, he presents a list of points and they often feel off, not because they're necessarily wrong, but because he only picks the ones that suit his "story". I suppose it's a Chinese thing, they've got serious censorship there, so his lectures must be censored by the government, and/or some self-censorship is going on there, and that's why many of his points feel weird.  
>   
> Like in the 3rd lecture, where he explains why the US went from industrial to financial economy - he mentions some of the popular explanations for that, and then he provides his own as "the real reason" (the transition to empire). ### **@translateislam574** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lkKrZq4YdqY&lc=UgwxMoS310lfCozTRC94AaABAg)

I have learned a lot through you - thank God, and I would like to give something back—something I believe is often neglected in your education system or not addressed objectively, neutrally, and truthfully due to ideological approaches. I’m speaking about an explanation of religion and religiosity.

1. First, we need to clarify two fundamental questions that form the basis for all further questions and matters. The first is whether there can be any other plausible explanation for our existence—and for the existence of all that we perceive or observe through empirical science—other than that there must be a being with the necessary attributes to bring all of this into existence. This being must not itself require a creator, must not be limited or influenced by anything—especially not by time and space. It must not get tired, must not sleep, must not possess the traits of relative beings like us, but instead be absolute in every aspect. If we pursue this question honestly and conclude—based on logic and reason—that no other acceptable explanation exists for our existence, then we arrive at the second question: has this being revealed itself to us? And if so, how? To what extent? And through which means? Especially in regard to fundamental questions such as why we were created.

2. Of course, I cannot fully answer these two questions here or present their evidence and reasoning in detail—this would exceed the limits of this platform. So, I will simply state that after decades of reflection, research, observation, and real-life application, I have come to the conclusion that the first question can only be answered by acknowledging that such a being must exist—one that possesses the necessary attributes to have created everything, without itself being created. All other theories, such as the universe always having existed or matter having emerged from itself, are irrational.

As for the second question, I have concluded that this being has revealed itself to us—at the very least—through the Qur’an. Its words and contents cannot possibly come from limited, fallible beings like us. Based on this, it follows that previous revelations in their original forms (not like in the Bible)—such as the Psalms, the Torah, and the Gospel—revealed to messengers like Adam, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus also originated from this one Creator and have always conveyed the same core message: that we were created to worship and serve the one and only Creator and to associate nothing and no one with Him—not other people, not ourselves, not our families or nations, and certainly not those in positions of power over us.

The purpose of human existence, which has been pondered and debated for millennia, is explained by this Creator in a single, yet deeply profound verse in the Qur’an:

"And I did not create the jinn and mankind except to worship Me."
(Surah Adh-Dhariyat, 51:56)

From the full context of the revelation, it becomes clear that our sole purpose is to worship God—and God alone. In this context, worship is not limited to obligatory acts such as the five daily prayers, fasting, or pilgrimage. In principle, every action can become an act of worship if done with the sincere intention to please God—so long as the act itself is permissible and not something prohibited (like drinking alcohol, dealing with interest, etc.). Even the manner in which a permissible act is done must align with God’s commands—for example, one may not cheat others in business, even if the product itself is lawful. Only when intention, permissibility, and method all align with divine guidance can an act be considered a true act of worship.

Unlike all other creations, which fulfill their purpose as designed, humans were given free will. We can choose to live according to the purpose for which we were created—or not. That is our test. Those who consciously enter this covenant with God and align their entire life with His will can expect reward in this life and, even more importantly, in the hereafter. Those who do not enter this covenant cannot expect such a reward. However, God has promised to provide sustenance to all people in this world—regardless of whether they seek the truth or acknowledge Him.

This way of living gives our lives true meaning—not one based on temporary, selfish gains, but rooted in living for the very reason we were created: to please our Creator, not people who are not superior to us in any way. Such a life gives meaning and fulfillment in this short-lived world and hope for an eternal reward in the hereafter. But the greatest reward is not paradise itself—it is the sweetness of willingly submitting one’s will to God’s will, being grateful for His priceless gifts such as the blessing of existence itself, and fulfilling the very reason we were created.

No worldly or even heavenly reward can match the inner peace and sweetness a person feels when they live in alignment with their Creator’s will. This feeling is an immaterial gift from God, and it cannot be attained through any other means. If disbelievers could experience this sweetness without having to enter faith, they would do everything in their power to taste it or to take it away from those who do.

I hope this helps to clarify why religion is not—as it is often portrayed—merely about seeking individual, worldly, or selfish benefits. It is far deeper and far more meaningful than that.

And I am saddened by the fact that I lack the power, the language, and the eloquence to convey this reality in a way that truly does it justice.

Nonetheless there are some glaring gaps and inaccuracies that damage my confidence in his perspective overall. One especially alarming statement was his assertion that Catholicism discourages people from reading the Bible. This couldn't be further from the truth, the Church "forcefully and specifically exhorts all the Christian faithful... to learn the surpassing knowledge of Jesus Christ, by frequent reading of the divine Scriptures. Ignorance of the Scriptures is ignorance of Christ." CCC133 (That's from the Official Catechism of the Church). Indeed generally the professor's statements along the lines of "That's all we know about Jesus Christ is..." in this video before jumping to what "Believers" claim happen is blurry and lacking depth of research. It might be all he knows about Jesus Christ, but it's a very, very long way from what the deposit of faith knows and humanity as a whole. He implicitly dismissed even the Gospel accounts  themselves as ahistorical, whereas not only are they themselves four separate eye-witness accounts but there's archaeological evidence, written evidence from non-Christians, and on-going evidence in way of more contemporary miracles (apparitions, Eucharistic Miracles, healings) that further verify their historical validity. How does the professor separate the two? History from belief?

Many comments here express admiration for the professor’s material. While I respect him personally, his lectures only skim the surface of geopolitics — a subject unfamiliar to many viewers. To be clear, I’m not formally trained in the field either. Still, these insights feel superficial at worst and merely introductory at best.

That said, I have gained something from these lectures. But what I would truly value is a comprehensive analytical framework for assessing a country’s geopolitical position — beginning with geography, history, and culture, and culminating in more tangible factors like economic and military power.

Ideally, such a framework would follow a structured process: assess internal factors, model outcomes, then repeat the same for neighboring states and the broader international system. Even at a glance, the complexity is immense. My respect goes to those capable of mapping such dynamics coherently.

I'm finding these videos just like everyone else is, and while I find them fascinating and very informative, I do feel the need to chime in about his comments on Dispensationlism.

I would encourage anyone to simply Google the word for a more clear definition. You can look and find, but you'll be hard pressed to see much explanation of Zionism in the definition. The word actually gives explanation and insight into the ideology. 

Dispensation:
1. noun

exemption from a rule or usual requirement.

The sum of the ideology is that believers view the Bible with literal interpretation. This is not unique to many Christian subsects. What is more characteristic is the idea that different people throughout time had different means towards salvation. And that the message of deliverance is dispensed based on the time(era), respective to cultural practices, and the group of people.

Meaning, that Pre-Flood humans descendant from Adam and Eve had a different expectation, than the Hebrews who were driven out of Isreal by the Babylons. And that the Hewbrews who were given the 10 Commandments had a different set of guidelines than the Isrealites who were given the sum of the Law during the era of Kings and Judges.  In Abraham's time the heart and spirit of a man, and the action's that resulted from this spirit, is how they are being judged. Whereas  Dispensationlist believe that faith alone in Jesus's death, Resurrection, and Godhood provides salvation to Christians, which logically couldn't be an expectation of Hebrews under bondage in Egypt, who pre-dated Christ's birth by multiple millennia. 

I think the previous explanations are enough for the average theologically illiterate person to comprehend how that might sound different to many Christians, who simply believe that faith in Jesus is the sum of salvation. And while true for them, that leaves for very little thought into how their personal salvation differs from some rando who lived 12,000 years ago. 

But, to explain how the Crucifixation of Jesus had a different impact on Jews compared to Gentiles, from a Dispensationlist view, compared to more mainstream Christianity, would take more nuanced. But, simply, the Promises given to Abraham, Issac, and Jacob are not yet to be fully realized; also, Jesus said HE is not here to break the Law, but fulfill it, thus we do not believe the new contract, provided to Gentiles through the Gospel, supercedes or breaks the Covenant already promised to Jews. No, Gentiles are exempt from the burden of the Law through Jesus and that work is done. But, we are still for when Jews as a nation will come back into subserviance under HIM,  with that being realized in a 1000 year Kingdom, with Jesus ruling a Holy Monarchy.

Which gets to where the video aligns, because we believe that God and Jesus have told us when this time will come about, and that we are called to watch for the signs of HIS coming.

Due to interpreting the Bible literally, we do believe that the time and place of His arrival is already stated, but that only when the right time is at hand, and the message is dispensed to the right people, will it be understood.  But, this idea that Christians need to DO or Fullfill certain obligations is absolutely not a core value or even a prominent idea. It's in fact quite anti-thetical. Or, to give the teacher grace, he may be be speaking on even more niche-sects of Christianity, maybe a specific subset or division of a group who also follows Dispensation, and also hold these views as well.  What he is suggesting is more a core ideollogy of Lucifarianism,  or certain sects of Jews who follow the Kaballah , or older Gnostic religions. Like, this is almost some Nordic Ragnarok type of ideology. Again, if you Google anything about Dispensationalism, you wont find a lot of emphasis on Zionist ideology.

It's moreso that we view Jesus as King, HE has a war coming, and HE's given us HIS battle plans. They aren't guidelines we need to follow or accomplish in order for HIS success. No, it's to make sure WE are ready when called. Or, it's like Jesus is coming. He has a flight that will be landing soon. When? Who knows? At some point. One day. Maybe tomorrow. But well...Acktually... HE has told us, albeit cryptically, when he will arrive. It's like he has given a map using symbols, that we need to decode. Is this necessary to ensure HIS safe arrival? No! It's to make sure that we can be at the right arrival gate when HE gets here.



So to be clear, while there are Christians who believe what he is describing. The group who hold these beliefs as core to their ideology are not called Dispensationlist.

Wheras many Chrisitans perceive this as metaphor. Or we believe that Lucifer ruled a pre-Adomic civilization before the creation of humans.
  
> 
  
> Another example of Dispensation, and the idea of a different message for a different people, is the telling of Genesis 6:12 that states:   
>   
> "And God looked upon the earth, and, behold, it was corrupt; for all flesh had corrupted his way upon the earth."  
>   
>   
> This passage is speaking on the entire population of humans being wicked, save Noah and his family. Now any Human who has read this bible verse, prior to the last ~150 years, would have only one way to interpret this verse. With the words flesh to mean humanity as a whole being corrupted through their behavior and actions. A more moral and spiritual implication. But, in our era, with the discovery and knowledge of DNA, and with the technology to manipulate one's genetics, now this verse has the potential to have an entirely different application. A meaning that no human prior to the discovery of DNA could possible have considered.  
>   
> In our current era, we could speculate whether these Pre-deluvian humans, who were interacting with the Sons of Man, or fallen angels, were doing some manner of genetic alteration. Combining Human DNA with that of other genus like fungus, plants, animals, insects, potentially viruses would be a way in which human "flesh" is corrupted. When GOD kicked Adam and Eve out of the Garden of Eden, HE promised Satan that his end would come from the line of Eve, one of the reasons there's many passages giving the lineage of Adam to Jesus. A strategy for counteracting this would be to somehow end that genetic line though a form of manipulation.  
>   
> Now whether this is what actually occurred isn't the point. The point is how that Bible verse can have a different message, for a different era of people, who have an entirely different understanding and comprehension. The idea that it is the same message, nothing about the integrity of it has changed, only our understanding. And, as we learn more about AI, dark-matter, quantum states, etc I'm sure we will come into new perspectives of comprehension.

> Which again, has nothing to do with acts-based works being a requirement to fulfill some end-time prophecy.

For instance, the claim that Zionism is only about 20 years old and that Christian Zionism came first is not right. The Balfour Declaration (the British Government communique supporting the creation of the state of Israel) was addressed to the Zionist Federation of Great Britain and Ireland (established in the 19th century), itself led by Jews. 

I think it’s fair to say that the Zionist movement gained significant popularity after WW2 and the Holocaust given Israel was seen to be the only safe place in the world for Jews (having experienced centuries of persecution and pogroms after being “blamed” for the death of Jesus, as portrayed in Mel Gibson’s Passion of the Christ.)

1) Christians who believe that if they can get all the Jews back into Israel, Jesus will return

2) Jews who believe that if they can get all the Jews back into Israel and all other religions out, it will usher in the end times

### **@Quezecotl2012**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lkKrZq4YdqY&lc=Ugy3M-iDgVeeUPKs9wl4AaABAg)

Christian Zionism supports the idea that Jews should return to Israel. It ties this to Bible prophecies about the end times. People with this view often back the modern state of Israel for religious reasons.
Dispensationalism links most strongly to Christian Zionism. It teaches that God has separate plans for Israel and the church. Israel gets a key role in the future. This includes a literal kingdom where promises to Abraham come true. Many dispensationalists see Israel’s 1948 founding as prophecy fulfilled. They push for Christian support of Israel to speed up end times events.
Premillennialism relates through its focus on a literal kingdom after Christ’s return. Historic premillennialism sees some future for Israel but views the church as fulfilling many promises. It offers moderate support for Zionism. Dispensational premillennialism ramps this up with a pre-tribulation rapture and Israel’s central spot in prophecies.
Amillennialism shows little tie to Christian Zionism. It views the millennium as symbolic and now. The church replaces Israel in God’s plan. Old Testament promises apply spiritually to believers, not literally to ethnic Jews or the land. This leads to less support for Israel as a nation in end times.
Postmillennialism has a mixed relation. Some versions expect Jews to convert and return to the land before a golden age. But most see the church fulfilling Israel’s role through gospel spread. Support for Israel focuses on conversion, not a political state. Newer forms link to social justice and sometimes oppose Israel.
Roman Catholic doctrine follows amillennialism. It sees the church as the new Israel. No special future exists for ethnic Israel beyond conversion to Christ. This view does not back Christian Zionism’s push for Israel’s role in prophecies.

People pick based on how they read Revelation 20.

### **@Quezecotl2012**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lkKrZq4YdqY&lc=UgzsDkLPfPUx4qtLWHh4AaABAg)

Dispensational premillennialism and historic premillennialism both affirm a literal return of Christ before a 1,000-year earthly kingdom. They expect a period of tribulation before that return. They include two resurrections separated by the millennium and a final judgment afterward
They differ on the church’s role in Old Testament prophecy. Historic premillennialism sees the church as part of those prophecies.
Dispensational premillennialism keeps the church separate from Israel and views the church age as an unexpected break in God’s plan for Israel.
They split on the rapture’s timing. 
Historic premillennialism places it after the tribulation so the church endures that period.

Dispensational premillennialism sets a pre-tribulation rapture to remove believers before the Antichrist’s rule.

They handle God’s plans differently. Historic premillennialism views one unified plan where the kingdom starts at Pentecost through the Spirit.

Dispensational premillennialism sets two plans with distinct roles for Israel and the church.

They vary on origins. Historic premillennialism traces back to early church fathers like Irenaeus and Justin Martyr. Dispensational premillennialism developed in the 1800s through John Nelson Darby and the Scofield Bible.

They interpret prophecy in distinct ways. Historic premillennialism allows flexibility beyond strict literalism. Dispensational premillennialism applies literal readings to all prophecies and expects Old Testament promises to Israel fulfilled exactly in the millennium.

People pick based on how they read Revelation 20.

The only thing I'd like to mention is that St. Basil the Great and St. John Chrysotrom were older than Augustine, and the fact that he misses the entire Orthodox Christian perspective, which is the true church, is kind of annoying, because it would clearly refute and show what Christians believed originally, so much so, that they put it in their liturgies that they still practice to this very day. You can clearly see this in regards to the Nicene Creed, and how the Catholics had internal struggle between those who wanted to change the creed and those who wanted to maintain the ancient creed. Orthodox Christians recite the ancient creed every single Sunday, which explains exactly what we believe. His overview of the "Catholic church ruling the world/having all power" is very one sided, and doesn't cover the true story, of how the Pope was excommunicated from the faith by the Eastern bishops, thus causing the split in the church, which came to a head 600 years after Augustine. For timescale, the Protestant Reformation didn't take place until the 1500s, 500 years after the Western("Catholic") church schism away from the ancient, true, original church.

My comment in no way takes away from a lot of the other information, but its very important that people learn that Orthodoxy is the original church, and you can see that via the liturgies that they still practice to this day, and the creed. I've enjoyed many of his lectures so far.

Side note, the bible doesn't teach you "not to drink, dance or have fun", that is nonsense puritanism. You think Jesus blessed a wedding with wine, but didn't drink any? They literally have wine during the Eucharist/communion. Paul tells his disciple to drink some wine for his health. It tells us not to get drunk. Why? Because its clearly not healthy for us, physically, mentally, spiritually. 

Also, the King of England never sent them to America to encourage them to start their own religions and government, it was a newly discovered land that was mostly untamed, and various peoples from different countries all came to this "new world" for all sorts of reasons, whether it was to live free, convert the natives, new opportunities, control more land, escape religious persecution, etc.
